     **(ii)** Security Groups for Pods on the data tier, so the database itself admits only those pods; **(iii)** both;
     **(iv)** accept it. *Recommendation*: **(i) now**, widening the edge's and the telemetry pipeline's tasks, because it
     costs each a small policy and needs no real environment; (ii) when a real environment exists. **Decided 2026-11-09**: as
     recommended; the telemetry half was built on 2026-11-11, and T4's and T15's rows are its evidence.
   - **(b) A pod's first moments on EKS.** The VPC CNI's agent defaults to `standard` mode, in which a new pod is default-allow
     until its policies are programmed. `strict` mode reverses that, for every pod in the cluster, so a pod with no policy has no
     network at all, kube-system's included. *Recommendation*: keep `standard` until a real environment can measure it: the window
     is seconds, at the start of a pod that has not yet served anything, and strict mode's failure is an outage. Revisit with the
     first real cluster. **Decided 2026-11-09**: as recommended; T4's row stays a `GAP` held by this item.
27. **T13's residual: the instance clocks outside the five stores** *(added 2026-11-09)*. The stored-bounds rule reads every
   production source's SQL, and classifies each comparison of a timestamp column with a bind parameter; it cannot say whose
   instant a bind is, and it cannot see a bound judged in Java. Read by hand, two of what it surrounds do judge a stored
   bound on an instance's clock. **Routing's version in force** (`findVersionInForce`, read with `Instant.now(clock)` at the
   payment confirmation and the withdrawal): a version's start is floored on the database's clock, but this read is the
   instance's, so within the skew of a scheduled start a payment can be routed by the outgoing or the incoming version. **The
   dispute door** (`DisputeActs.requireRespondable`, from the response and the evidence upload), in Java, so outside the
   census: a stored `respond_by` judged on the instance's clock, so within the skew of the deadline a response can be
   refused or admitted early or late. The census also names instance clocks that place a cutoff or a window and decide
   no bound: a sweep's `UNKNOWN` arm and settlement's parse and acceptance backoff (the cutoff picks candidates or paces a
   retry, and a conditional write re-decides), a dispute-deadline gauge's horizon, and risk's rolling look-backs (a skewed
   instance counts a window shifted by its skew). *Recommendation*: leave the candidate, pacing and gauge cutoffs where they
   are; accept the look-backs as a count and not a bound; and move routing's read and the dispute door to the database's
   clock with their next change, before production use (one read each, as the card fee version's was). Routing selects a
   rail and prices nothing, and the door's window is the skew against a deadline the network set in days. *The alternative*:
   schedule the two as their own task now. **Decided 2026-11-09**: as recommended; the two reads are `PI-021`, required before
   production use, the cutoffs stay and the look-backs are accepted as a count.
28. **T38's remainder: the rows a caller can add by being refused** *(added 2026-11-09 by the task that built the wider
   audit class)*. A refused or failed act at a privileged door is one audit row per attempt, as a denial and a refused
   step-up are, and a retry is a second attempt. Reaching the door takes an authenticated holder of its permission or an
   active merchant key; the row names them and holds nothing they wrote, so a row costs the caller a request and tells on
   them. Nothing per caller limits the rate. A merchant key's limit is already owed by the abuse-limits task (ADR-0052). The
   options for an operator: (a) accept: a named, permissioned population; every row of a flood is its own evidence, and the
   privileged access review reads them; (b) a per-identity limit on the same cluster-wide counter the abuse-limits task
   builds, which a row at the limit would still record. *Recommendation*: (a), and (b) only if the review ever finds it
   used. Held by a `GAP` row in §4. **Decided 2026-11-09**: (a).
23. **T7's, T9's, T10's and T32's residuals: what a plan of the data tier cannot settle** *(added 2026-11-09 by the data-tier
   modules' gate; numbered for the tasks that ran beside it)*. Three questions, none yet asked of a real account.

   **(a) Where a backup under a separate key lives.** The plan's phrase, automated backups under a separate key, is not
   something RDS offers: its backups and snapshots carry the instance's key, and within one account and region AWS Backup's
   vault key does not change that. So a separate key means a copy that crosses a boundary, and each root requires that
   copy's destination. The options: a dedicated backup account in the same region, a copy under that account's key at a daily
   recovery point; replicating automated backups to another region, which alone keeps point-in-time recovery under another
   key and is Phase 16's multi-region work; or accepting that the backups share the volume's key. *Recommendation*: the
   dedicated backup account. It answers the threat that matters most, an attacker or a mistake inside the production account
   reaching the backups along with the database, and takes nothing from Phase 16. Add the cross-region replication there if
   a recovery point under the separate key tighter than a day is required. **Decided 2026-11-09**: the dedicated backup account,
   when an account exists.

   **(b) The audit archive's lock.** Its period is `OPEN` until a real jurisdiction is chosen, and its mode is the same
   kind of choice, so both are required inputs with no default. Neither root can be planned for real until someone chooses.
   *Recommendation*: when a jurisdiction is named, S3's `COMPLIANCE` mode in production, because it is the only mode nobody in
   the account can shorten, which is what "write-once" has to mean against an insider, and `GOVERNANCE` in staging, where a
   wrong period must stay correctable. A period set in the stronger mode cannot be undone for an object once written, so
   decide it before Phase 16 writes the first one. **Decided 2026-11-09**: as recommended, the period and the choice of
   jurisdiction still open.

   **(c) The path that applies the ACLs.** They are declared as resources, so a plan shows them, and applying them needs
   what no apply here has needed: a runner inside the network holding the provisioner's credential, and three applies in
   order, the middle one with ACLs not yet enforced, because MSK has no super user who could write the first one.
   *Recommendation*: run it from the same pipeline role item 7 settled, from inside the cluster's network, and finish the
   three stages in one sitting. **Decided 2026-11-09**: as recommended, when an account exists.

22. **T3's, T31's and T1's residuals at the edge** *(added 2026-11-09 by the edge's review)*. Two separate things.

    *(a) An operator's credentials on the public host.* The doors that issue a session are public, because a customer needs them, and
    nothing refuses a role-holding identity there. So an operator's password works from the internet; a phished password or session can
    elevate (`POST /v1/authentications/mfa`), enrol a factor (`POST /v1/me/mfa`) or change the password (`POST /v1/me/credential`) from it;
    `POST /v1/recoveries` is public alone; and because T1's lockout counts per identity, anyone on the internet can lock out a named
    operator. Using such a session on an operator route still needs the internal network. The control would be: the public edge sets a
    surface marker it strips from clients, the application refuses a role-holding identity on the public surface, and the lockout's
    counter is settled against that (per identity and surface, or refused before it counts). That is new behaviour on the authentication
    path. *Recommendation*: a task of its own in M15.7, not the abuse limits. Those count unauthenticated traffic by source; this
    decides, by identity and surface, who may authenticate at all, with its own audit and negative tests, and there it would be buried
    in a different acceptance. It should follow the abuse limits, whose counter it needs for the lockout arithmetic only.
    **Decided 2026-11-09**: as recommended, as a task of its own in M15.7. *Built 2026-11-11*: each edge marks the surface, staff are
    refused on the public one at every door that issues or strengthens access, before the lockout counts, and recovery for staff is on
    the operator host alone; T1's, T3's and T31's rows carry the evidence, and what it leaves is item 39. The abuse limits' counter turned
    out not to be needed: refusing before the count settles the lockout with no new store.

    *(b) The spool under more than one source.* One source cannot fill a controller's 256 MiB spool (130 MiB at the in-flight limits and
    the body cap), but several can, and an abandoned upload's spool is held until the proxy's next garbage collection (under two minutes
    measured on the pinned image). While the spool is full, a body past 64 KiB answers `500` and everything smaller is served; no pod is
    evicted or restarted. *Recommendation*: accept it for Phase 15 as a bounded degradation, and give a real environment a connection
    limit or WAF in front of the edge, sized by Phase 16's load model. **Decided 2026-11-09**: accepted for Phase 15, as
    recommended; T1's row stays a `GAP` held by this item.

33. **Two residuals of the abuse limits** *(added 2026-11-09 by the abuse limits)*.

    *(a) T38's remainder: a per-key limit on the merchant API.* ADR-0052 left the rate limit per
    API key to Phase 15, and T38's `GAP` row is what follows from it: a key can add one `door.ActRefused` record per refused request, and
    nothing per key limits how many. What bounds it today is the edge's machine tier, which is per source and per controller, so a
    merchant with several addresses has several budgets; the rows carry the key's public id, so a runaway key is visible and revocable.
    The counter now exists (`platform.window_counter`), so what is missing is the number and the contract: a per-key limit is a
    commercial term as much as a safety one, the task that built the counter excluded merchant quotas, and a `429` on a merchant route is
    a change to the published merchant contract. *Recommendation*: keep this a `GAP` for Phase 15 and add a per-key limit from the existing
    counter with the first real merchant, once that merchant's expected traffic gives the number. If you want a bound sooner, a per-key
    limit on *refused acts only* is the smallest one: it bounds exactly these rows and never touches a merchant's accepted traffic.

    *(b) T1's remainder: a shared address is locked out, not merely shared.* The limits are per address. Sign-in's 100 per 15 minutes counts
    successes too, so behind a carrier's NAT or an office, one device retrying spends the budget in seconds and everyone there is
    refused for the rest of the window; the 11th new customer from an address in an hour waits up to an hour. The numbers' basis is
    arithmetic and no traffic, because none exists: registration's 10 an hour bounds T47 and is ten times what one person needs; sign-in's
    100 per 15 minutes is a few people retrying at once; recovery's 10 an hour bounds the mail one source can send. *Recommendation*:
    accept it for Phase 15, with the alert, and let Phase 16 calibrate these from a load model. If the first real launch serves a market
    where carrier NAT is the norm, the change to make is to count sign-in's *failures* per source rather than its attempts. That has a real
    cost, because the count would then come after the derivation it exists to bound, so it needs its own decision.
    **Decided 2026-11-11**: as recommended: (a) stays a `GAP` until the first real merchant gives the number; (b) is accepted for
    Phase 15, Phase 16 calibrating the limits from a load model.
31. **T6's and T29's residual: what a plan of the security modules cannot settle** *(added 2026-11-09 by the task that built them)*. Five
    things, none needing a decision before an account exists:
    (a) **Who installs a cluster's first controllers.** People hold two groups and no access policy, deliberately, so nobody can
    create anything on a new cluster, including the operator and the GitOps controller that do the rest. *Recommendation*: the pipeline
    role of item 7, given an access entry with `AmazonEKSClusterAdminPolicy` for that one step and removed afterwards, by a reviewed
    change, and recorded with the apply. Never a person, and it would need the rule refusing an access policy to name that exception.
    (b) **The first fill of every entry.** Terraform creates each entry empty and holds no value (`OPERATIONS_RUNBOOK.md` §11.7 is the
    procedure, never run, a fixed version token making a second write fail rather than replace). No role in this repository can write a value. *Recommendation*: the same pipeline
    role, one run per environment, holding `secretsmanager:DescribeSecret` and `secretsmanager:PutSecretValue` on these entries and `kms:GenerateDataKey` on their keys, and nothing that reads.
    (c) **A trail of the account.** `PHASE_15_PLAN.md` section 10's audit column counts on Secrets Manager's and KMS's access logs, which are
    the account's API trail, and no module creates one. AWS's event history keeps ninety days and nothing more. *Recommendation*: an
    organisation trail into `log-archive` under the `archive` key, when an account exists, beside item 7.
    (d) **Whether the key policies are right.** Every one is unverified (the `security` module's README). *Recommendation*: apply to staging
    first and read the failures, and only then production.
    (e) **A person's way in.** The baseline refuses access keys, so a person has no base credential for a shell session, and `GetSessionToken` needs one; the console assumes a role
    and has no `kubectl`. Nothing routes to the private endpoint or the database either. So break-glass, the read-only role and the bootstrap step are correct and cannot be
    used. It fails closed. *Recommendation*: workforce federation (C13) when a workforce identity provider exists, with a network route to the private endpoint planned beside it, and no sign-in path built before that.
    **Decided 2026-11-11**: as recommended, each when an account exists; (e) stays a `GAP` until workforce federation (C13).

32. **T34's residual: what a ring's rotation cannot hold by itself** *(added 2026-11-09 by the key-ring task)*. Three things.

    *(a) A version's value replaced after it was listed.* Nothing in code can tell that a slot's stored value changed while a
    version kept its number, and overwriting a listed version's value makes every row under the old value **permanently
    unreadable**, because no key anywhere can decrypt it once the old value is gone: replicas holding either value read as the
    same version and fail at each other's authentication tags, indistinguishable from tampering. The runbook forbids it
    (section 11.29). A per-version check value in the ring's non-secret configuration, a short keyed digest of a fixed
    label, would refuse the replica at startup, at the cost of a value a person must publish. *Recommendation*: add it
    when the rotation rehearsal writes the first version's value on a cluster, which is the first act that can get this wrong.
    *(Built 2026-11-11 by the rotation rehearsal, and seen refusing on a cluster: T34's rows. What it leaves, a ring of one
    version with no check value, is item 36 (b).)*

    *(b) Credit's ring cannot retire a version from this code.* `credit.credit_evidence.key_version` is not readable by the
    application's role, so a retirement on that ring refuses at startup, always. Retiring needs a one-line grant of that
    column, a number and nothing the row's ciphertext could not already show. *Recommendation*: take the grant when a
    retirement is first wanted, which is C12's purge decision.

    *(c) That a rollout finished before a version was activated.* A replica cannot start without every listed version, so a
    finished rollout proves the fleet holds it, and CI refuses a change that activates a version its base did not list.
    Neither can see that the first rollout completed before the second change landed: the runbook states that gate. The
    wrong order loses nothing, because reads of the new version are refused until the replica that cannot read them has
    rolled. *Recommendation*: accept until the delivery applies manifests through a GitOps controller, which can hold the
    second change until the first has rolled.
    **Decided 2026-11-11**: as recommended: (a) is the rotation rehearsal's, whose entry was widened; (b) waits for C12's purge decision; (c)
    is accepted until the delivery holds it. *(Built 2026-11-12, and not by the controller: Argo CD syncs the newest commit of the branch,
    so two commits merged inside one polling interval reach a cluster as one sync. The hold is at the merge instead: in staging and
    production the activation check refuses the change until that environment's newest smoke record shows every replica on one
    configuration listing the version, T34's row.)*

34. **T20's residual: a release that lengthens the session idle timeout** *(added 2026-11-09 by the configuration register's task)*.
    `SessionPolicy` is a constant, so no overlay can change it and a fleet holds two values only while a release that changes it rolls
    out. During that rollout, and after it, a touch extends a live session by the idle timeout of the instance that touches it. The
    extension is monotonic and stays below the absolute bound copied at issue, which no touch can exceed. So a release that lengthens
    the idle timeout lengthens sessions already issued, up to that bound. A release that shortens it reaches sessions already issued
    within one old idle window: a touch cannot pull an expiry back, but it stops extending one past the new bound. No financial outcome or limit depends on it, and it needs a release, so a reviewer sees it. The options:
    - **(a) Accept it** until the idle timeout next changes.
    - **(b) Store the idle bound on the session at issue** and extend by the stored bound: one identity column, one migration, one change to
      the touch.

    *Recommendation*: **(a)**, reviewed the day the constant changes, and (b) as part of that change: nothing is planned to move the
    constant, and a column that protects against a change nobody is making waits at no cost. The register's section 6 states the behaviour.
    **Decided 2026-11-11**: (a), (b) to come with the change that moves the constant.

30. **T2's, T4's, T22's and T33's residual: which plugin enforced the policies** *(added 2026-11-09 by the rehearsal)*.
    The denials those rows name have run on one machine, whose Linux kernel (Docker Desktop's) has no `CONFIG_NFT_QUEUE`. kindnetd
    cannot enforce a policy there, so the rehearsal brought kube-router in its firewall-only mode, and its record names the engine
    that answered. CI's `rehearsal` job uses kindnetd and has not run yet. A managed cluster's plugin is a third implementation of the
    same objects, and neither of these speaks for it.
    - **(a) Accept it for Phase 15** and read the first CI run of the job: a denial that does not hold there is a defect to fix, and
      its artefacts say which engine answered.
    - **(b) Rerun on a machine whose kernel has the module** before the phase exit, so that kindnetd has answered once before CI does.

    *Recommendation*: **(a)**. The job needs no machine anyone has to find, and every denial sits beside a control, so a plugin that
    enforces nothing goes red rather than green.
    **Decided 2026-11-11**: (a); the first CI run of the `rehearsal` job is read for which engine answered.

35. **T27's and T30's residual: the production signing identity, the namespaces admission does not watch, and what a real
    environment's admission needs** *(added 2026-11-11 by the signing task; parts (c) to (f) by its review)*. Six separate things.
    - **(a) Who signs in production.** CI signs today under a key pair generated for each run, which proves the path and
      vouches for nothing past the run, because no registry or Sigstore identity exists. The options:
      - **(i) Keyless, from CI's own identity.** GitHub's token issuer, the subject this workflow on `refs/heads/master`,
        and the policy naming exactly that identity, never a pattern. `id-token: write` is granted on the signing job
        alone, and `CiWorkflowIsLockedDownTest` names that one exception. Fulcio and Rekor are the public instance unless
        the organisation runs its own.
      - **(ii) A key held in KMS** (ADR-0122's alternative). Someone owns it and its rotation, and no transparency log is
        needed.

      *Recommendation*: **(i)** with the public instance, applied when the registry exists. Then a signature names the
      commit's workflow run and no key has a holder. A private instance waits for an organisation that has one.
    - **(b) The namespaces admission does not watch.** The policy controller's own namespace, External Secrets' and
      `finapp-observability` run third-party images. Each is pinned by digest in its manifest, and nothing at the cluster
      enforces it. Opting a namespace in refuses all of it, because no policy names those images. The options:
      - **(i) Accept it until a registry exists**, then mirror each pinned upstream digest into it, sign the mirror under
        the CI identity, and opt the namespace in.
      - **(ii) Name each image's digest as an exception now.** This widens the exception list from one entry to a list
        that grows with every controller.

      *Recommendation*: **(i)**. The digest pins hold what they can today, and an exception list that grows is the
      defect admission exists to prevent.
    - **(c) Rotating the trusted key or identity.** A pod is admitted only under an authority the policy holds now. After a
      rotation, a new pod of a digest signed only under the old key is refused: a rescheduled replica, an HPA scale-out, and
      the rollback target the delivery keeps. The options:
      - **(i) Overlap**: the policy holds both authorities, and every digest still running or kept for rollback is signed
        again under the new one, before the old authority is removed.
      - **(ii) A clean cut**: remove the old authority at once, and accept that a rollback past the rotation is a forward fix.

      *Recommendation*: **(i)**. A rotation must never take away the way back, and re-signing a digest changes no byte of it.
    - **(d) Who may write the signature repository.** Its tags are mutable by design, so whoever can push there can delete or
      replace a signature or an attestation. That denies service: every pod of that digest is refused. It could also undo a
      revocation by restoring a removed signature. It cannot forge one: the controller verifies each signature's digest and each
      attestation's subject against the image (cosign's `SimpleClaimVerifier` and `IntotoSubjectClaimVerifier`). The options:
      - **(i) Only the pipeline's signing role writes it**, nobody reads it but the clusters' pull identities, and its writes
        are in the cloud's audit trail.
      - **(ii) Immutable tags there too**, which makes a second attestation on a digest impossible.

      *Recommendation*: **(i)**, and a revocation becomes an authority change (part (c)), never a deletion alone.
    - **(e) A TUF outage, under part (a)'s public instance.** The controller initialises its trust root from the public TUF
      mirror when it starts, and stops if it cannot. While its replicas restart in that outage, `failurePolicy: Fail` refuses
      every new pod in both namespaces. The options:
      - **(i) A trust root of our own** (the controller's `TrustRoot` resource, or `-tuf-root` with a mirror we run), refreshed
        on a schedule.
      - **(ii) Accept it**, with two replicas and a disruption budget making a restart of both together rare.

      *Recommendation*: **(i)** when keyless is applied. Admission should not depend on a third party being reachable at the
      moment a pod starts.
    - **(f) The controller's own registry identity.** In a real cluster the controller reads signatures, attestations and image
      configurations from the registry. In AWS that needs a role of its own with read access to the image and signature
      repositories, and the security modules hold none. Without one, every image is refused. The options:
      - **(i) A read-only workload identity for the controller**, scoped to the environment's repositories, added to the
        security module.
      - **(ii) Image pull secrets**, a long-lived credential.

      *Recommendation*: **(i)**, beside the two workload identities that exist, never a stored credential.

    **Decided 2026-11-11**: as recommended, each when a registry and a real cluster exist: (a) (i), keyless signing from CI's own identity
    on the public instance; (b) (i), the three namespaces unwatched until then, each pinned upstream image then mirrored, signed and its
    namespace opted in; (c) (i), overlapping authorities, every digest running or kept for rollback signed again before the old one goes;
    (d) (i), the signing role alone writes the signature repository, and a revocation is an authority change; (e) (i), a trust root of
    our own when keyless is applied; (f) (i), a read-only workload identity for the controller.

36. **T6's, T7's, T9's, T22's, T34's, T36's and T44's residuals: what the rotation rehearsal leaves** *(added 2026-11-11 by the rotation rehearsal; (e) to (g) by its review's fix round)*. Seven things.

    *(a) ADR-0032's rotation policy for a customer's credential, and its "older than X" report.* ADR-0032 gave Phase 15 both, and no
    register row held either. Built: a customer's credential changes when its holder changes it, which ends every other session, and its
    derivation moves to current parameters on its next successful use. Not built: a schedule, and the report. What the report would
    watch is a database disclosure's cost (T7): a credential under weaker parameters is cheaper to guess offline. *Recommendation*: no
    scheduled rotation of a customer's credential, which changes on the holder's act or on suspected compromise; and the report as the
    count of credentials under parameters older than current policy, the upgrade's convergence, postponed until the first change of
    parameters, because until then every credential is under the first ones and it would read zero.

    *(b) A ring of one version with no check value* (T34). A replica refuses a stored value that does not give its published check value,
    for every version of a ring holding more than one (a second version listed without one for each refuses) and for any version given
    one. A ring of one version with none starts as it always has, so a base key overwritten in the store before the first rotation is
    still found only by its first row failing. The first fill now publishes one (`OPERATIONS_RUNBOOK.md` section 11.7), but nothing
    requires it. *Recommendation*: require one for every held version off loopback once a real environment exists, so that a replica
    cannot start without one; until then, publish it at each environment's first fill.

    *(c) A provider's API credential and a report source's* (T22). Their method is the callback secret's, two valid during a window
    (`SECRET_MANAGEMENT.md` section 5.1), but the window is the provider's to hold: the simulated provider verifies no credential, so a
    rotation against it would pass whatever was sent. *Recommendation*: rehearse each against the provider's own sandbox when a real
    provider is connected, which plan section 1.1 defers with every real provider.

    *(d) Three classes not rotated on the cluster* (T6). The migrator's credential (between releases: the role's password and the store
    change, and the next Job reads it), the source-bucket key (one store change and a roll, every source starting a fresh window once)
    and the master credential (the managed service's own mechanism). None moves money or needs a window, and the first two use steps the
    rehearsal ran for other classes. *Recommendation*: rehearse the first two in the operations rehearsal's run of runbook section 11.7,
    and leave the master credential to the environment, as section 5.1 of `SECRET_MANAGEMENT.md` does.

    *(e) The upgrade path into the check value* (T34). A replica of this release refuses to start when its ring lists two versions and
    publishes no check value for each. An environment already listing two, rotated before this release, would see every new replica
    refused while the old ones serve on: closed, not open, and the rollout stalls rather than completes. No environment exists yet, so
    none is in that state. *Recommendation*: before this release reaches an environment whose ring lists two versions, compute each held
    version's check value from the store with `infra/scripts/KeyCheckValue.java`, as runbook section 11.29's step 0 computes them, and
    add `…_RING_CHECKVALUES` in the same overlay change that carries the release; if a rollout has already stalled, add them and roll
    again, the refused replicas holding nothing.

    *(f) The PostgreSQL and Kafka CA bundles* (T9, T36). `PHASE_15_PLAN.md` section 10's TLS row includes the bundles a replica verifies
    the database and the brokers against. The rehearsal renewed the edge's certificate; neither bundle was changed under a running fleet,
    and in a real environment both authorities are the managed services'. *Recommendation*: rehearse a bundle change (both old and new
    authorities in the bundle, roll, then the old one out) when a managed service first announces one, in the operations rehearsal.

    *(g) The reporting pool refuses a writing role by name* (T44, the review's M8). It refuses `finapp_app`, its two alternating users,
    the migrator and the superuser by name; a role holding a writer's rights under any other name starts it. *Recommendation*: a
    capability check at the pool's start, the role refused if `pg_has_role(current_user, 'finapp_app', 'USAGE')` or any
    `has_table_privilege(…, 'INSERT')` on a ledger table answers true, beside the name check; not built in the rotation rehearsal.

    **Decided 2026-11-11**: as recommended: (a) no scheduled rotation of a customer's credential, ADR-0032's report the count of
    credentials under older parameters, postponed until the parameters first change; (b) a check value required for every held version
    off loopback once a real environment exists, published at each first fill until then; (c) the provider credentials rehearsed
    against each provider's sandbox when one is connected; (d) the migrator credential and the source-bucket key in the operations
    rehearsal's run of runbook section 11.7, the master credential the environment's; (e) the check values carried in the same overlay
    change as the release; (f) a CA bundle change rehearsed when a managed service first announces one; (g) the capability check at the
    reporting pool's start, built 2026-11-12: on every connection the pool makes, `MEMBER` of `finapp_app` (which a `USAGE` check would
    miss for a role only able to `SET ROLE` to it) or of the server's program and file writers refused, and, by the role, `PUBLIC` or
    any role it is a member of, outside the system schemas: `INSERT` or `UPDATE` on any column (a column grant is invisible to
    `has_table_privilege`, which the first version asked, the review found), `DELETE`, `TRUNCATE` or `TRIGGER` on a table, `UPDATE` or
    `USAGE` on a sequence, `CREATE` on a schema (§4's T44 rows). Functions are not judged: the platform's `SECURITY DEFINER` functions
    are executable by `finapp_app` alone.

37. **T46's residuals, and the trace's last join: the telemetry back ends of a real environment, the reconciliation leg, and amounts in
    exception messages** *(added 2026-11-11 by the telemetry pipeline's task; (d) to (f) added by its review's fix round, the same day)*.
    Six decisions.
    - **(a) The back ends.** Traces and logs reach Tempo and Loki on `kind` and in compose only, each holding seven and thirty-one days. A real
      environment's back ends have readers of their own, a retention of their own and an identity to write with, and the infrastructure
      module already plans a `logs` and a `traces` bucket per environment that no role reads or writes. The options: **(i)** Tempo and Loki
      in the cluster on those buckets, each with its own workload identity reaching its own bucket alone, read by operators through the
      operator surface's identity; **(ii)** a managed tracing and logging service, reached by the collector as one more exporter; **(iii)**
      decide nothing until an environment exists.
    - **(b) The reconciliation leg.** A run decides an item in the ingesting file's correlation, and its domain spans open before the leg
      restores even that. So no span joins a reconciliation run to the payment it settles: each of the payment's requests is a correlation the
      platform mints (ADR-0034) holding its own legs (the request, the provider call, the posting, the outbox, every consumer), the file's upload and
      attestation are two more, the intake's spans
      carry the file's identifier and the run's the source, and the stored chain (expectation, allocation, item, run) is the join, as
      `PHASE_8_PLAN.md` section 15 decided the durable trace would be. The options: **(i)** keep the stored chain as the join; **(ii)** open
      the settlement and reconciliation spans inside the correlation each leg restores, and record a marker span in the expectation's
      stored correlation, caused by the run, when a run settles it: one more span per matched item, and a change to the matching leg.
    - **(c) Amounts in exception messages.** Read with ASM's analyser, thirteen production classes (`Money` itself among them, and the
      merchant fee, FX conversion and refund classes) put an amount into an exception's message for a state that must not happen, or
      into a Lombok builder's generated form; and `BigDecimal` figures appear in others' messages the analyser cannot tell from any
      number. Such a message reaches no error body and no exported span, but an unhandled one is printed with its stack trace: the
      collector scrubs `Money`'s string form from the shipped record, and the pod's console keeps it. The options: **(i)** rewrite each
      message to name the rule and the currency, never the figure, and suppress the two builders' forms - a change to sixteen classes'
      messages and the tests that read them; **(ii)** accept it, the set held by the build so it cannot grow - held per instruction,
      thirty-eight of them, so a new one inside a listed class is a change to the list in the same review.
    - **(d) The telemetry stack's own alerts.** The collector counts what it drops and refuses, and Tempo and Loki count their own
      failures, but no rule reads any of it: a collector that has refused every batch for an hour, or a back end that stopped ingesting,
      is seen only by someone looking. `PRODUCTION_READINESS.md` lists it as not done. The options: **(i)** rules on the collector's
      `otelcol_exporter_send_failed_*` and `otelcol_receiver_refused_*` rates and on each back end's ingest, in the alert catalogue with
      runbook sections; **(ii)** leave telemetry unwatched, since no money depends on it.
    - **(e) Erasure against the logs' retention.** A person's erasure (C12) reaches the database's rows, but a log record that printed
      something of that person stays in Loki for its thirty-one days, and the platform has no way to find or remove it there. The
      options: **(i)** keep the logs' retention short enough to be the erasure's own bound, stated in the retention schedule as such;
      **(ii)** a deletion request per person against the log store, which needs a person's identifiers in the logs to find them by -
      the opposite of what the scrub aims at; **(iii)** a log store per retention class, the shortest for anything a person's request
      could name.
    - **(f) The operator surface's trace context.** The public edge removes a caller's `traceparent`, `tracestate` and `baggage`, so a
      public caller can neither switch tracing off for its own requests nor choose the trace ids the collectors route by. The operator
      edge keeps them, for staff's tooling, so a member of staff can do both for their own requests: an operator's work can go
      unsampled when that operator chooses. The options: **(i)** remove them at the operator edge too, and let staff's tooling find a
      request by the correlation id every answer carries; **(ii)** keep them, the operator surface being staff's alone and every act
      audited whatever the trace says.

    *Recommendation*: **(a)(i)** with the first real environment, at seven and thirty-one days unless a jurisdiction says otherwise:
    it keeps the telemetry inside the account the platform already secures, and the buckets exist. **(b)(i)** now, and (ii) only if an
    operator ever needs the span join: the stored chain is durable and audited, a span is sampled telemetry, and a change to the matching
    leg in a phase that moves no money needs a reason this does not give. **(c)(i)**, as one small remediation entry before production
    use: a figure in a message helps nobody fix an overflow, and the console is a sink the collector cannot scrub. **(d)(i)**, with the
    first real environment and in the runbooks' rehearsal of section 11: a second net nobody watches can fail for a week.
    **(e)(i)** now: thirty-one days is already the logs' class, so the retention schedule says it is also the erasure's bound for
    anything a log printed, and (iii) only if a regulator asks for less. **(f)(i)**: staff's tooling loses nothing the correlation id
    does not give, and a trace no member of staff can switch off is the simpler thing to audit.

    **Decided 2026-11-11**: (a), (b) and (c) as recommended: (a) (i) with the first real environment, at seven and thirty-one days
    unless a jurisdiction says otherwise; (b) (i), the stored chain is the join; (c) (i), as `PI-022`, required before production use.
    **Decided 2026-11-12**: (d), (e) and (f) as recommended: (d) (i), the stack's rules written and rehearsed on `kind` by the
    runbooks' rehearsal (built 2026-11-12), live with the first real environment; (e) (i), the logs' thirty-one days stated in
    `DATA_RETENTION.md` as the erasure's bound for anything a log printed, a store per class only if a regulator asks for less;
    (f) (i), the operator edge removes them too, built 2026-11-12 (§4's T46 row): staff's tooling finds a request by the
    `X-Correlation-Id` its answer carries, which neither edge touches.

38. **T32's residual: the managed restore, and a logical restore into a managed instance** *(added 2026-11-11 by the restore
    rehearsal; (c) added the same day by its review)*. The rehearsal restores a logical backup into a PostgreSQL it runs itself
    (`OPERATIONS_RUNBOOK.md` section 11.35). Three things it cannot settle.

    **(a) The managed restore.** A point-in-time restore makes a new instance. Its parameter group, security group and subnet group
    must be attached before anything connects (`infra/terraform/modules/postgres`, its README's "Operating it"). A physical restore
    brings the platform's roles back able to log in, so the master user must make all three `NOLOGIN` before any address of the new
    instance is given to anything, and the verification must run from inside the network as the master user, acting as the schemas'
    owner. Today the verification is a command on the repository's test classpath, not a Job of the application image, and no account
    exists to run any of it. *Recommendation*: rehearse it in the first real environment before it holds a customer's money, with the
    verification packaged as a Job of the application image, and record it beside section 11.35's record; until then nothing claims a
    managed restore.

    **(b) A logical restore into a managed instance.** It cannot move the transaction counter, because `pg_resetwal` needs the stopped
    server's data directory and a managed service gives no one that access (the service's model; AWS's own pages were not read for
    this), so the source-join census cannot see the restored journal and the verification refuses the restore. The options:
    - **(i) Keep it refused.** A managed restore is always physical (point in time or snapshot), which carries the counter with it.
    - **(ii) A reviewed migration resetting the census head to its floor.** It still leaves every restored entry above the counter
      unwalked until the counter passes it, which for a cluster in a later epoch is never.

    *Recommendation*: **(i)**, as section 11.35 states it, because (ii) cannot make the walk whole.

    **(c) GL = ledger over a locked period on a restore has run only over none.** The first period the platform opens is the
    database's current month (`AccountingPeriods.openNext`), and a period locks only once `LockGrace`, at least two business days,
    has passed since its end on the database's own date (`AccountingPeriodStore#today`). The seeded source is created the day the
    rehearsal runs and the `kind` rehearsal's database the hour it runs, so neither holds a locked period unless the database's clock
    is faked, which a rehearsal of a restore must not do: both of section 11.35's records, the seeded source's and the `kind`
    rehearsal's database's, judged GL = ledger on an open period only, through the same `GlPins.forPeriod`. What holds the locked case today is narrower: `GlPinsTest` holds that a locked period is
    read by its pin and one without a pin is refused, and `RestoreVerdictsTest` that a period the verification cannot read refuses
    the restore. *Recommendation*: rehearse it at the first month end of the first real environment, a restore of a backup taken
    after its first period locked, as part of (a)'s rehearsal and recorded beside section 11.35's record; until then nothing claims
    GL = ledger was shown over a locked period on a restore.

    **Decided 2026-11-11**: as recommended: (a) the managed restore rehearsed in the first real environment before it holds a customer's
    money, the verification a Job of the application image; (b) (i), a logical restore into a managed instance refused; (c) GL = ledger
    over a locked period rehearsed at that environment's first month end, as part of (a).

39. **T3's and T31's residuals once staff are refused on the public surface** *(added 2026-11-11 by the task that refuses them;
    (a) rewritten and (c) added by its review's fix round, the same day)*. Three things, each a decision.

    *(a) A session that predates the role, or one stolen.* The doors that issue or strengthen a session read the grants on every
    request, so a role granted mid-session makes the next public door refuse its holder, and a revoked one lets it serve them. A session
    that already exists keeps working at the level it has: on its holder's own customer routes from the internet, and on every operator
    route from the internal network that its level satisfies. **The level is all the interceptor checks**: `requireAssurance` reads the
    session's assurance, with no freshness and no record of where it was issued or elevated. So two cases reach operator routes that
    need `MULTI_FACTOR`:
    - **(i) A customer's session elevated on the public surface before a first role.** A customer with a factor steps up from the
      internet, as customers may; a role granted afterwards turns that session into a staff session at `MULTI_FACTOR`, which reaches
      every `MULTI_FACTOR` operator route from inside.
    - **(ii) A stolen session already elevated on the operator surface.** Whoever holds it, from inside, holds what the operator holds.

    The options:
    - **(a1) Accept both**: each needs a session and the internal network.
    - **(a2) End the grantee's sessions when a first role is granted**, so every staff session was issued at a door that knew the role:
      one call in the grant, its record, and the grant door's tests. It closes (i). It leaves in place the recovery channels and factors
      the identity added as a customer, from the internet, before the grant: they keep proving the identity on the operator surface.
    - **(a3) Record the issuing and elevating surface on the session**, and refuse a public-issued or public-elevated session at an
      operator route: an identity column, a migration and a check in the interceptor. It closes (i) as well, and survives a grant without
      ending anyone's sessions.

    Nothing in (a1) to (a3) closes (ii): a stolen operator-surface session from inside is T3's standing assumption, answered by the
    session's own bounds (idle and absolute expiry, revocation) and by every operator act's record, not by the surface.

    *Recommendation*: **(a2)**, with the grant's review also confirming the grantee's recovery channel and factor (`PRIVILEGED_ACCESS_REVIEW.md`'s
    review of holdings is where that belongs). It closes (i) at the cost of one sign-in for a newly promoted person and needs no schema
    change. (a3) is the stronger rule and is worth its migration if stolen-session replay from inside becomes a concern of its own.

    *(b) The two signals nothing reads.* If the application reaches a cluster before the edge sends the marker, staff are refused at
    those doors and customers are served; each refusal is recorded `surface=unmarked` and counted under
    `finapp.identity.surface.refusal{kind="unmarked"}`, and the runbook says the edge goes first (`OPERATIONS_RUNBOOK.md` §11.26). A
    refused sign-in whose password was the operator's own is recorded `credential=proven` and counted under
    `finapp.identity.surface.refusal{type="authentication",outcome="credential_proven"}`: a phished operator password arriving from the
    internet. No alert reads either series. *Recommendation*: a **page** on any `outcome="credential_proven"` refusal, since it is a
    compromised credential in use now, and a **ticket** on any `kind="unmarked"` refusal in 10 minutes, so the first locked-out operator
    is not how a rollout mistake is found; both added with the next change to the platform's alert rules (the telemetry pipeline's task
    is in flight on them).

    *(c) Staff who are also customers.* An identity holding a role is staff at every door that proves it, so a staff member who banks
    on the platform with the same login cannot sign in from the internet to their own wallet: they sign in on the operator surface, from
    the internal network, and use their customer routes there. The codebase models a role-holder acting as a customer on their own
    account; the surface rule takes the internet away from that person. Nothing records it beyond the refusal itself.
    - **(c1) Accept it**: staff use their own accounts from inside, or hold a second, customer-only identity for the internet.
    - **(c2) Let a role-holder sign in on the public surface to a session that can never act as staff**: a session-level flag and a
      check at every operator route, which is (a3)'s machinery under another name, and a password that works from the internet again.

    *Recommendation*: **(c1)**, with a second, customer-only identity as the supported way to bank from the internet. (c2) gives a
    phished operator password back its internet sign-in, which is what this control exists to take away.

    **Decided 2026-11-11**: as recommended: (a) (a2), a grantee's sessions ended when a first role is granted, the grant's review
    confirming the grantee's recovery channel and factor, and (b) the page on a proven refusal and the ticket on unmarked refusals;
    a stolen session elevated on the operator surface stays T3's standing assumption; (c) (c1), a second, customer-only identity the
    supported way for staff to bank from the internet. **Built 2026-11-12** (§4's T3 and T31 rows): (a2) in the grant's own
    transaction, a second role ending nothing; the review's question is `PRIVILEGED_ACCESS_REVIEW.md` §5 question 6, first asked at
    the review's first performance. One case (a2) leaves: a public sign-in that read the grants just before a first grant committed and
    inserted its session just after, which the revocation's row lock makes wait and cannot see. That session is `PASSWORD`, no public
    door strengthens it, and from inside it reaches nothing the password it came from does not, so no owner item is opened for it; it
    would be closed by the public sign-in reading the grants again after its session's insert, which waits on that lock, and refusing.
    (b) pages on any proven refusal, by kind, so an unmarked one during a rollout pages beside the ticket, as ruled.

40. **T27's residual: where the signatures live, and the scanner's finding on it** *(added 2026-11-12 by the delivery's task; revised
    the same day by its review's fix round)*. Item 35 (d) and ADR-0122 decision 3 put each environment's signatures in a repository
    of their own whose signature tags are mutable, because cosign adds a second signature or attestation to a digest by rewriting that
    digest's `sha256-<digest>.sig` or `.att` tag. The pinned Trivy, which CI's `iac` job runs over the infrastructure code and which
    fails on HIGH (`PHASE_GATES.md` §5 Phase 15 criterion 2), refuses a mutable repository: `AWS-0031 (HIGH): Repository tags are
    mutable`, seen 2026-11-12 on that one resource with the pinned version. Ignoring it there would be a scan finding accepted, which
    only the owner does. The review asked for ECR's `IMMUTABLE_WITH_EXCLUSION`: every tag immutable except those matching an exclusion
    filter. Evaluated the same day: the pinned AWS provider accepts it with one `sha256-*` wildcard filter, which is exactly cosign's
    tag form and nothing a promotion writes; **the pinned Trivy still reports `AWS-0031` for it**, its rule reading the mutability
    setting alone and not the exclusion. The options:
    - **(a) Accept `AWS-0031` on a fully mutable repository, with an `until`**;
    - **(a') `IMMUTABLE_WITH_EXCLUSION` for `sha256-*`, and accept `AWS-0031` on that one resource with an `until`**: the same
      finding, on a repository in which no tag but cosign's can move;
    - **(b) Immutable tags there too**, which refuses a second attestation on a digest (item 35 (d)(ii));
    - **(c) Keep it out of this repository**, in the environment's own infrastructure beside its access entries.

    *Recommendation*: **(a')**, until 2027-06-30, the date T42's acceptance runs to: the mutability of cosign's tags is the design,
    and the scanner's rule does not read the exclusion. **Built that way**, so that the ruling needs no further change: the registry
    module's `signatures` repository, its run in `tests/registry.tftest.hcl`, the inline ignore with that expiry on that one resource
    (past it, the `iac` job fails again), and each environment root's `signature_repository_url` output, which the admission policies
    and `promote.sh` read. Who may push there stays item 35 (d): whoever can can delete or replace a signature, which forges nothing but
    refuses every pod of that digest, and the grant to the signing role alone is a repository policy, which needs an account. The image
    repositories' untagged-expiry rule stays out until a promotion lands under an immutable tag, which is the pipeline role's
    cross-account copy (`OPERATIONS_RUNBOOK.md` §11.36).

    **Decided 2026-11-12**: (a') as recommended, under the owner's standing word for waves G and H: `IMMUTABLE_WITH_EXCLUSION` for
    `sha256-*` on the signature repositories, AWS-0031 accepted on them alone until 2027-06-30, as built; who may push there stays
    item 35 (d).

42. **T3's, T12's, T14's, T15's, T18's, T23's, T28's, T29's and T33's residuals: what the runbooks' rehearsal leaves** *(added 2026-11-12 by the
    runbooks' rehearsal)*. Eleven things, each a decision; (d), (e), (j) (i) and (k) built the same day.

    **(a) The age alerts read the oldest alone** (T23). `BreakAgeHigh` pages on `max by (severity)
    (finapp_reconciliation_break_age{severity="high"}) > 86400`, and `SuspenseItemAged` raises a ticket on
    `max(finapp_reconciliation_suspense_age_seconds) > 2592000` (`infra/prometheus/rules/settlement-reconciliation.yml`). Each series
    is the age of the oldest open break of its severity, or of the oldest open suspense item, so each rule is one alert. Parked value
    with no attributable owner (`OPERATIONS_RUNBOOK.md` §11.11) is a HIGH break and a suspense item that stand until a later payments
    phase's operation pays the value out. While one stands, both alerts stay firing on it, and a new HIGH break or a newly aged item
    behind it raises nothing new: the alert on-call already has is the only one they get. `BreakAgeMedium` and `BreakAgeLow` read the
    same way; parked value stands at HIGH. Runbook §11.11 step 4 has a person read the open HIGH breaks and the suspense register
    every working day meanwhile. The options:
    - **(i) Count what is past its bound.** A gauge per severity of the open breaks older than that severity's bound, and one of the
      suspense items older than thirty days, read in the same refresh as today's series, counts and never an identifier or an amount
      (ADR-0072); and beside each of today's rules, one that fires when its count rises, so a newly aged break or item raises an alert
      of its own while the oldest stands. Two meters, two rules and their promtool cases; nothing reconciliation decides changes.
    - **(ii) Keep the rules, and §11.11's daily read.** Nothing built: the next HIGH break is found by a person's habit rather than a
      page, for as long as parked value stands.
    - **(iii) Leave parked items out of what the alerts read** (an assigned break with its note, or the causes §11.11 names). It
      silences the standing alert, and with it the alert on a parked item nobody is working. Suspense is never a resting place
      (`INV-REC-05`), and this would make it one.

    *Recommendation*: **(i)**, built in Phase 16 before any environment holds parked value, this item holding the gap until then, with (ii) the control meanwhile. No
    environment holds parked value yet; once one does, it stands for months, and an alert that started firing months ago says nothing
    of the break raised today. (iii) hides value from the alert meant to see it. **Decided 2026-11-12** under the owner's standing
    word ("accept all recommendations", waves G and H): as recommended.

    **(b) A migration outside a transaction** (T12, T33). The build refuses one: `EveryMigrationRunsInATransactionTest` reads every
    script with the pinned Flyway's own parser and refuses each form Flyway would run outside a transaction (`CREATE INDEX
    CONCURRENTLY` and the others its PostgreSQL parser names, `executeInTransaction=false` beside a script, and the setting made for
    every script at once). The failed-migration rehearsal showed why (runbook §11.4): on the pinned Flyway with PostgreSQL's default
    transactional lock, such a migration that fails leaves no row in the history and leaves its effect (an `INVALID` index), every
    later run is refused by that leftover, and `flywayRepair` has nothing to remove. Only the schema's owner dropping the leftover
    clears it, and in a deployed environment no person can (item 31 (e)). What stays open is a reviewed migrator mode for such a
    migration, and the price of doing without one. The options:
    - **(i) Keep the build's refusal until a real need arises**, every index built in its migration's transaction, as ledger `V029`
      builds one. A plain `CREATE INDEX` blocks every write to its table for as long as it builds; the migrator's `lock_timeout`
      (3 s) bounds the wait for that lock, not the build once it holds it. On a large live table that stops the table's writes for
      the build's length, which nothing has measured, and no table is large yet.
    - **(ii) A reviewed mode in the migrator**: Flyway's per-session lock (set through the pinned Flyway's
      `PostgreSQLConfigurationExtension`), under which a failure leaves a `success = false` row refusing every later run until an
      audited repair removes it, the files known good and the reason recorded; or an audited drop of a leftover, its lock bounded.
      Either needs a person's way in (item 31 (e)) to read what was left and decide, and the rehearsal found that a per-session lock
      moves, rather than closes, the gap a run stopped mid-way leaves (§11.4).
    - **(iii) An audited operational procedure ahead of the release**: the index built by a person, `CONCURRENTLY`, before the release
      whose migration then finds it. The same way in, and an index the history does not record until that migration runs.

    *Recommendation*: **(i)**, until a table's transactional build is measured and its write stop found unacceptable (Phase 16's load
    characterisation measures that); then (ii) in the migrator or (iii), whichever the measurement favours, through item 31 (e)'s way
    in. A mode that can leave a failed row or a leftover in production needs a person to finish it, and nobody can today; the refusal
    costs nothing until a table is large. **Decided 2026-11-12** under the owner's standing word ("accept all recommendations",
    waves G and H): as recommended. Runbook §11.4 cites this part.

    **(c) The access review over a real environment's holders** (T3, T18). `PRIVILEGED_ACCESS_REVIEW.md` §5's review has been
    performed only as a rehearsal, on `kind`, over the rehearsal's own identities (its performances table). Over a real environment's
    holders it needs that environment, and two things more. An environment's first holding is written around the door (the first
    administrator, `README.md` §5e), so it has no grant record and is asked every question as if it had one; and that write needs a
    person with database access. And the review's read of the holdings is not a door: the live rows of `identity.role_assignment` and
    their `identity.RoleAssigned` records are read from the database, and question 5's factorless-holdings read is the only one the
    application serves. In a deployed environment no person reaches the database today (item 31 (e)). The options:
    - **(i) The first performance before the environment holds a customer's money**, once its first roles are granted, the holdings
      read through item 31 (e)'s way in, in a read-only session; then at the owner's cadence, each performance recorded in §5's table
      by the reviewer and a second person.
    - **(ii) A read door for the review**: the live holdings with their grant records, on a `MULTI_FACTOR` session, audited as the
      factorless-holdings read is, so the review needs no database session. A new standing read of every staff holding, for a human
      procedure whose cadence the owner sets.
    - **(iii) The first performance at the owner's first scheduled review after go-live**, leaving the first administrator's holding,
      and whatever was granted while the environment was set up, unasked until then.

    *Recommendation*: **(i)**. An environment's first holdings are the ones no door recorded, so the review that reads them comes
    before money does; and the way in it needs is the one runbook §11.12's review query and §11.39's reads already wait on.
    **Decided 2026-11-12** under the owner's standing word ("accept all recommendations", waves G and H): as recommended.

    **(d) An administrator's suspension of the last administrator** (T3). The role-revocation door refuses to take a role that can
    grant roles from the last `ACTIVE` identity holding one (`409 identity.LastRoleGranter`). The suspension door had no such rule, so
    an administrator could suspend the last other administrator (a suspended holder does not count: it cannot sign in), and leave
    nobody able to grant, revoke, suspend or reinstate through the platform's doors. The options:
    - **(i) The suspension door refuses the last `ACTIVE` identity that can grant roles**, as the revocation does, and risk's
      suspension is not bound by it.
    - **(ii) Bind risk's suspension too**: administration always survives, and a compromised last administrator cannot be stopped by
      risk.
    - **(iii) No rule**: the suspension stays the way round the revocation's refusal.

    *Recommendation*: **(i)**: where availability and security meet, security wins, so risk can still suspend a compromised last
    administrator. **Decided 2026-11-12** under the owner's standing word ("accept all recommendations", waves G and H): as
    recommended. **Built 2026-11-12**: `POST /v1/identities/{id}/suspension` refuses a subject that is the last `ACTIVE` identity
    holding a role that can grant roles, `409 identity.LastRoleGranter`, nothing written. Like the revocation, it locks the subject's
    row and then every live holding of a granting role, in key order, before the status write, and counts the holders by a fresh
    statement after the locks, so two administrators suspending each other at once, or a suspension racing a revocation, leave
    exactly one (`LastAdministratorSuspensionTest`; the database cases owed). Risk's automated suspension, a party case's
    `SUSPEND_IDENTITY` proposed by its investigator, approved by a second risk analyst and carried out by the platform, takes none of
    these locks and is not refused, so a compromised last administrator stays suspendable. It can leave no administrator: by taking
    the last one, or by racing an administrator's suspension of the other of the last two, whose locks it never waits on. That is this
    part's residual. Administration then comes back by one of two ways. The first is risk's own: where the holder can be trusted
    again, a `REINSTATE_IDENTITY` on a case of that party (opened by hand if the first is disposed), proposed and approved by two risk
    analysts, four eyes, the approval on a stepped-up session, after which the identity is `ACTIVE` with its roles and no session,
    and runbook §11.12 step 6 follows. The last is the out-of-band write of `README.md` §5e, by someone holding a database credential
    that can write `identity`'s tables: a granting role inserted for an `ACTIVE` identity, or the suspended one's `status`,
    `suspension_cause = NULL` and `status_changed_at` written together; where the holder cannot be trusted again (an attacker's factor
    on it, (g)), it is the only way. In a deployed environment no person has that credential today (item 31 (e)).

    **(e) The administrative doors' step-up** (T3). The grant and the reinstatement served a password session: a grant widens every
    other authorization decision, and a quiet reinstatement is how an accomplice undoes an incident response. So did the suspension,
    so a stolen administrator's password could suspend every other administrator, the responders among them, down to the last, which
    (d) keeps and which is then the thief's own. The role-revocation door already stepped up. And a password alone may enrol an
    identity's first factor, so a factorless administrator's stolen password could enrol the thief's factor and then pass every
    `MULTI_FACTOR` door. The options:
    - **(i) Step up all three, and make `ADMINISTRATOR` need an enrolled factor**, as the roles that can redirect a merchant's money
      already do.
    - **(ii) Step up the grant and the reinstatement, and leave the suspension on the session its administrator holds**: quicker to
      stop an identity, and the stolen password still suspends every other administrator.
    - **(iii) Leave all three as they were.**

    *Recommendation*: **(i)**. It is in tension with a principle the platform holds elsewhere, "stopping is the fail-safe direction"
    (`PRIVILEGED_ACCESS_REVIEW.md` §6's second ruling), by which a kill switch's stop stays one person's act on the session they hold.
    A suspension stops an identity, and a responder on a password session is now refused (`403 identity.AssuranceRequired`, recorded
    `identity.StepUpRefused`) until they step up. It is accepted because a suspension takes access away from another person and ends
    their sessions, where a kill switch's stop halts a flow; because an administrator granted through the door now holds a factor,
    so the step-up costs a genuine one seconds; and because what stops money stays quick: a kill switch's stop still needs no step-up. **Decided 2026-11-12**
    under the owner's standing word ("accept all recommendations", waves G and H): as recommended. **Built 2026-11-12**:
    `POST /v1/identities/{id}/roles`, `POST /v1/identities/{id}/suspension` and `DELETE /v1/identities/{id}/suspension` declare
    `MULTI_FACTOR` beside the revocation, and their refusals are recorded; a grant of `ADMINISTRATOR` (decided from what the role
    grants: granting roles, suspending identities) is refused `409 identity.FactorRequiredForRole` until the subject has a confirmed
    factor (`RoleGrantNeedsAFactorTest`), and §5 question 5's factorless-holdings read names a factorless administrator too. The
    risk desk's approver (`RISK_ANALYST`, holding `RISK_CASE_APPROVE`, which approves a suspension exempt from the last-administrator
    rule) joins the same rule the same day.
    `PRIVILEGED_ACCESS_REVIEW.md` §6's third ruling records the tension. A holding written around the door has no such check: a
    factorless administrator from before the rule, or written in by `README.md` §5e without a factor, reaches no administrative door
    until they enrol, which is self-service on a password session, so the review's check is what finds them.

    **(f) Recovery delivers no token** (T3). `POST /v1/recoveries` starts a request whose token is never in a response and is
    delivered nowhere (`RecoveryController`), and no channel's verification challenge is delivered either. The notifier that would
    deliver them is a business capability: `PHASE_15_PLAN.md` §25 re-dated it, and §22 forbids Phase 15 to add one. `DECISIONS.md`
    §Deliberately Deferred names its owner only as "the phase that adds a channel notifier", and no plan names that phase:
    `ROADMAP.md`'s phases include none for notification, and `CAPABILITY_MAP.md` lists "Notification" and "Account recovery"
    with no phase. So no recovery completes today, a member of staff's or a customer's. Runbook §11.12 step 6 is the way round it for
    a compromised operator credential: if the holder still knows the current password, they sign in at once on the operator host, step
    up with their own factor and change it (`POST /v1/me/credential`, which ends every other session; an identity with a factor cannot
    change its password on a password session, `403 identity.AssuranceRequired`); otherwise the person registers a new identity under
    a different login. The options:
    - **(i) Keep that fallback until the notifier's phase.**
    - **(ii) An administrator's reset of another identity's credential**: no channel needed, and a door by which one administrator's
      stolen session takes over any identity. Phase 13 ruled that the platform never resets a credential, even in answer to an account
      takeover (its C10, ADR-0112).
    - **(iii) A notifier in Phase 15**: a business capability, which §22 forbids.

    *Recommendation*: **(i)**. A new identity costs a person a login and keeps the old one's history whole; a reset door would make
    every identity as safe as the least safe administrator's session. **Decided 2026-11-12** under the owner's standing word ("accept
    all recommendations", waves G and H): as recommended, until the phase that adds a channel notifier, which no plan names yet.

    **(g) No door removes a confirmed factor or a verified channel** (T3). `MfaFactorStatus` has no state past `ACTIVE`, and no door
    or store method reaches a confirmed factor (`PRIVILEGED_ACCESS_REVIEW.md` §6). Its holder cannot replace one either: replacing a
    confirmed factor needs a `MULTI_FACTOR` session, which only that factor gives. A verified channel is never replaced (`INV-IDN-06`:
    a second verification is refused `409 identity.VerifiedChannelAlreadyExists`), and changing one waits for the notifier. So a
    compromised identity carrying an attacker's factor, or a channel its holder does not recognise, cannot be cleaned and reinstated:
    runbook §11.12 step 4 leaves it suspended for good, and the answer is a new identity. No challenge is delivered today, so no
    channel can be verified in a deployed environment yet; a channel becomes this case when the notifier arrives. The options:
    - **(i) Keep it**: the old identity suspended with its history and its login taken for good; the person registers again, enrols
      a factor, and is granted roles afresh, each grant with a reason naming the incident, the first ending the sessions it held as a
      customer, and the next review asking its question 6 of the new identity.
    - **(ii) An administrator's door removing a factor or a channel from a suspended identity**, reasoned, stepped up and audited,
      refusing removal while a role needing a factor is held, as `PRIVILEGED_ACCESS_REVIEW.md` §6 requires of any removal. The
      identity left with a password alone may enrol its first factor on a password session, so whoever holds the password races its
      holder again; and the door lets one stepped-up administrator disarm any identity's second factor.

    *Recommendation*: **(i)**, with removal re-dated beside the verified channel's change to the phase that adds a channel notifier,
    whose step-up, notice to the channel replaced and cooling-off a removal needs as much as a change does (`DECISIONS.md`
    §Deliberately Deferred). A new identity carries nothing the attacker added; its cost is one person's new login. **Decided
    2026-11-12** under the owner's standing word ("accept all recommendations", waves G and H): as recommended.

    **(h) No socket timeout on the database connection** (T14). `application.yaml` sets the driver's `connectTimeout` (5 s) and no
    `socketTimeout`, and says why: "It belongs with Phase 3, where the queries exist." That note is stale: Phase 3 is long complete,
    and the queries exist. After a database failover a connection the network black-holed never returns (runbook §11.40): a request
    or a tick holding one waits until the operating system gives the connection up, holding its thread and one of its pool's seven
    connections, and only replacing that pod frees them sooner. Two reads bound their own wait, the schema guard's (3 s) and the
    abuse counters' (2 s), each by a network timeout on its connection. The `statement_timeout` the code arms on other reads is the
    server's, and a black-holed connection never hears the server. The `kind` rehearsal cannot show the case: a stopped server resets
    its connections. The options:
    - **(i) A socket timeout on the data source now**, above the longest bound the code arms (the accounting checks' 30 s). Every
      statement that arms none gets an unmeasured bound, and one that runs longer than the guess fails every time it runs.
    - **(ii) A network timeout per schedule**, on the threads a held connection stalls longest, as the two reads above already set
      theirs, requests left to the pool. Narrower, and still a guess per schedule.
    - **(iii) Correct the note now, and re-date the socket timeout to Phase 16's failover drill**, which measures both the
      interruption and the longest statement under load. Until then, runbook §11.40 step 3: a replica still unready, or still failing
      requests once the others serve, is replaced by eviction under break-glass.

    *Recommendation*: **(iii)**. A held thread costs one replica part of its capacity, and a person can free it. A socket timeout
    below a legitimate statement makes that statement fail every time it runs, and a proof or a census that never finishes leaves the
    platform blind to its own correctness. The number is a measurement under load, which Phase 16 owns. **Decided 2026-11-12** under
    the owner's standing word ("accept all recommendations", waves G and H): as recommended.

    **(i) A corrupt card attempt stalls the whole sweep, and nothing pages** (T15, T23). The card sweep rehydrates its candidates'
    typed ids while listing them, before its per-row containment, so one `payments.payment_attempt.id` that is not a UUIDv7, on a row
    the sweep would take (dispatched, or unknown past its bound), fails every tick on every instance, and no candidate is reached
    (runbook §11.39 (a)). No door writes such an id: it is a raw writer's. The only sign is the schedule's WARN line, `Payment sweep
    failed: <class>`. `PaymentUnknownAged` reads `finapp_payments_unknown_age`, which counts `*_UNKNOWN` operations alone
    (`infra/prometheus/rules/flows.yml`), so a corrupt `*_DISPATCHED` attempt with nothing else unknown pages nothing; a page comes only
    when another attempt turns unknown meanwhile and ages past the rule's bound. The withdrawal, dispute-answer, return and pay-in
    initiation sweeps list the same way. The options:
    - **(i) A meter on a sweep's failed tick, with a page**: a counter per sweep where its schedule logs the failure, and a rule paging
      when it has counted in each of the last fifteen minutes, as BNPL's and risk's sweep claim-failure rules count theirs. It sees any
      tick that fails whole, a corrupt id or anything else.
    - **(ii) Bring Phase 16's change forward**: each listing skips and counts a row it cannot rehydrate, as the per-row loop does
      (`CURRENT_STATE.md`'s debt row, re-dated to Phase 16's resilience work). A change to every such sweep's listing.
    - **(iii) Leave it**: the WARN line, and the flows' own alerts once something else waits.

    *Recommendation*: **(i)**, built in Phase 16 beside (ii) and before production use, this item holding the gap until then, for the card sweep and each sweep runbook §11.39
    (a) names whose only sign is its WARN line; (ii) stays Phase 16's. A stalled sweep leaves money unattended, not misjudged, but the
    row behind it is a raw writer's mark and runbook §11.12's `SEV-1`, and an incident nobody is paged for is found late. **Decided
    2026-11-12** under the owner's standing word ("accept all recommendations", waves G and H): as recommended.

    **(j) A data-dependent migration that fails in production, with staging ahead** (T12, T33). A pair reaches production only after
    staging ran the same pair with a passing smoke (runbook §11.36), so a migration that fails on production's data, a new `CHECK`
    say, has already applied in staging. A fix ordered below it is then refused in staging (`outOfOrder = false`: `Detected resolved
    migration not applied to database`, rehearsed) and can never be promoted. Nobody reaches production's data to fix it by hand
    (item 31 (e)), and after a failed migrator hook §11.36 forbids syncing the same revision again. Production stays on N − 1,
    serving: the failed migration changed nothing, the release's earlier ones are expand-only or marked, and the rollout never
    started. But every later release carries the same migration, so production takes none until its data is fixed. The options:
    - **(i) A tightening migration's contract marker names the data fix that precedes it in the same release**, and the build refuses
      one that names none: a constraint never ships without the fix meant to make every row meet it. The fix is still written without
      reading production's rows, so a row it misses ends at (iii) all the same.
    - **(ii) A reviewed data-fix mode in the migrator**, running an audited fix carried in the release before the same revision runs
      again: a second path by which the migrator's credential changes financial data, beside the reviewed migrations it already runs
      (T33).
    - **(iii) Production stays on N − 1 until a person can reach the data**; the data is then fixed by an audited operational
      procedure written into the incident's record (§11.12), and the same revision synced once more by a reviewed manual sync,
      §11.36's one exception, which runbook §11.4 states.

    *Recommendation*: **(i) and (ii), (i) first**, with (iii) the way back meanwhile. (i) as a build rule: a tightening migration is
    added `NOT VALID` with its `VALIDATE` in a later release, or its contract marker names the data fix that precedes it in the same
    release, so a failure on data no environment checked stops appearing in production alone. (ii) as one reviewed SQL file run with a
    reason and the incident's id, audited, built with the first real environment beside item 31 (e)'s way in; until then a failure (i)
    did not prevent ends at (iii). **Decided 2026-11-12** under the owner's standing word ("accept all recommendations", waves G and H):
    as recommended. **Built 2026-11-12**: (i), the build refusing a tightening migration that is neither `NOT VALID` nor marked with the
    data fix preceding it, and failing closed: a statement in the three classes the expand-only lint cannot read that judges no row
    (`ALTER TABLE old OWNER TO …`, `CREATE STATISTICS`) is refused unless a data fix is named, and such a form needs the lint taught,
    not a marker (`DATA_MIGRATIONS.md` §4.1). No real migration above the floor is affected. (ii) is deferred to the first real
    environment.

    **(k) No person's role reaches the telemetry pipeline's namespace** (T28, T29). The human roles of `deploy/base/rbac-humans.yaml`,
    read-only and break-glass, are bound in `finapp` alone, so runbook §11.34's steps in `finapp-observability` (a collector's log, a
    back end's pods, a stuck pod evicted) were the cluster administrator's, an act no role bounds. The options:
    - **(i) The same two roles there**: a read-only Role (get, list and watch on pods, their logs, deployments and events) and an
      evict-only break-glass Role, bound to the same two groups, the manifest policy's human-role rules applied to that namespace's
      stream as to the application's.
    - **(ii) Leave the steps to the cluster administrator**, each recorded as a break-glass act is (runbook §11.25).

    *Recommendation*: **(i)**. A person reading a collector's log is an ordinary act, and an ordinary act that needs the administrator's
    access is one nobody bounds. **Decided 2026-11-12** under the owner's standing word ("accept all recommendations", waves G and H):
    as recommended. **Built 2026-11-12**: `deploy/observability/base/rbac-humans.yaml` (the two Roles and their bindings to the same
    groups); `infra/scripts/manifest-policy/finapp.rego`'s `[rbac-human-namespace]` widened to exactly the two namespaces;
    `observability.rego` reusing the `[rbac-` rules, with `[obs-break-glass-evictions]` and `[obs-person-reads]` (the exact reads);
    `edge.rego` reusing the `[rbac-` rules too; the collectors' disruption budget admitting an unready collector's eviction
    (`unhealthyPodEvictionPolicy: AlwaysAllow`); runbook §11.34's steps read as reads under the read-only role and a collector's
    eviction under break-glass. No person reads Prometheus, Tempo, Loki or Alertmanager, and the back ends are not break-glass's to
    evict.

## 7. Decided against

As `PHASE_15_PLAN.md` §9.3 decided, and kept here so that the model does not count these as missing controls:

- **mTLS inside the cluster, and a service mesh.** With one deployable there is no service-to-service hop. The hops
  that leave a pod (B4, B5, B6) each have, or are given by a Phase 15 task, their own TLS and authentication. Today
  B4, B5 and B6 each require verified TLS off loopback, and B5 a SCRAM identity as well (B5 since 2026-11-08, when
  the Kafka clients' authenticated TLS was built).
- **An API gateway product.** Authentication and authorisation are the application's (ADR-0030, ADR-0031). A gateway
  would be a second policy point that can disagree with the first. The edge does TLS, allow-lists and coarse limits.
- **OIDC for customers.** Customer identity is the platform's own product. Workforce federation waits for a workforce
  identity provider (C13).

---

## 8. What this register does not claim

- That a control is effective beyond what its evidence holds. A test proves its own assertions, and the control's
  text is written to them.
- That a `database`-tier test ran fresh on this tree (§4's note).
- That any planned control exists before its task's gate replaces the promise with evidence. The guard enforces that.
- That the platform is ready for production. That is `PRODUCTION_READINESS.md`'s question, and `P15-TSK-034` answers it.
- Compliance with, or certification under, any standard.
